--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 1 results:**

The champion (score 16818.78) uses a 2-state FSM:
- State 0: Spiral (B=6) with transitions on probability, high-neighbour, and low-neighbour conditions
- State 1: Repulsion (B=5) with transitions on black floor, neighbour counts, white floor

The second best (17586.15) is simply GoAwayColor from color 4 (yellow) - moving away from yellow walls. This is interesting but doesn't directly target blue walls.

The key insight for this mission: robots need to move TOWARD BLUE walls (color 3 = blue based on ARGoS color scheme, or we need to check). Looking at the best performers, GoAwayColor with clr=4 (yellow) scored well at 17586. We need to move toward blue walls. In ARGoS, colors are typically: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan.

**Strategy:**
1. **Elitism**: Copy champion verbatim
2. **Exploitation**: Small edits of champion (shift params ~10%)
3. **Exploration**: Try GoToColor with blue (clr=3), GoAwayColor from green (clr=2), multi-state FSMs combining color-seeking with floor detection

Key insight: Blue walls = upper strip (reward zone). So GoToColor with blue (clr=3) should work. GoAwayColor from green (clr=2) should also work. Let me build machines around these ideas.

For the color scheme in ARGoS/AutoMoDe: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan.

**14 machines:**
1. Champion (exact copy)
2. Champion variant - slight prob/gain tweaks
3. Champion variant - different repulsion gain
4. GoToColor blue (clr=3) - single state
5. GoAwayColor green (clr=2) - single state  
6. 2-state: GoToColor blue → stop on black floor
7. 2-state: GoToColor blue → GoAwayColor green with floor condition
8. 3-state: GoToColor blue, then stop on black, with GoAwayColor green backup
9. 3-state mixing color and exploration
10. 2-state: Exploration → GoToColor blue on color perception
11. 2-state variant of champion with GoToColor
12. 3-state with blue-seeking and neighbor conditions
13. GoAwayColor green + stop on black
14. Mixed approach with ZigZag and color seeking

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 2 results:**

Massive improvement! The champion dropped from 16818 to 3191 - a 5x improvement. The key discovery: **GoToColor blue (clr=3)** is the core winning strategy.

**Champion analysis:**
- State 0: GoToColor blue (B=8, clr=3) - moves toward blue walls
  - Transitions: black floor (p=0.7) → stop, sees blue (p=0.6) → stop, random (p=0.04) → stop
- State 1: Stop (B=1) - stays put
  - Transitions: gray floor → GoToColor, sees green (p=0.5) → GoToColor, random (p=0.02) → GoToColor  
- State 2: GoAwayColor green (B=9, clr=2) - backup
  - Transitions: sees blue (p=0.7) → GoToColor, black floor (p=0.6) → GoAwayColor (self-loop? No, → state 1)

Wait, state 2 has transitions: n2x0→state 0, n2x1→state 1. So it goes back to GoToColor when blue seen, and stops on black.

The second best (3617) is just a single-state GoToColor blue - remarkably simple and effective.

**Strategy:**
1. Copy champion exactly
2. Exploit champion with small parameter variations
3. Try 4-state extensions of champion
4. Try variations with different transition probabilities
5. Keep the simple GoToColor as a strong baseline

Key parameters to tune in champion:
- p0x0 (black→stop): 0.7, try 0.8, 0.85
- p0x1 (blue→stop): 0.6, try 0.7, 0.8
- p1x0 (gray→GoToColor): 0.3, try 0.2, 0.4
- p1x1 (green→GoToColor): 0.5, try 0.6, 0.7
- p2x0 (blue→GoToColor): 0.7, try 0.8, 0.9
- p2x1 (black→stop): 0.6, try 0.5, 0.7

Also: the stop state (state 1) has a transition back when it sees green (bad wall) - this means if a robot accidentally stops near green, it'll move away. Smart!

Let me also explore: what if the GoAwayColor-green state transitions directly to stop on black (rather than back to GoToColor)?

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 3 results:**

The top 5 performers are all very similar 3-state FSMs with the pattern:
- State 0: GoToColor blue (B=8, clr=3) with transitions on black floor, sees blue, [sees green], random
- State 1: Stop (B=1) with transitions on gray floor, sees green, random
- State 2: GoAwayColor green (B=9, clr=2) with transitions on sees blue, black floor

The champion added a 4th transition in state 0 (sees green → stop) and scored 2943 vs 2960 for the 3-transition version.

**Key patterns across best performers:**
- p0x0 (black→stop): 0.70-0.90, sweet spot ~0.75-0.80
- p0x1 (blue→stop): 0.60-0.80, sweet spot ~0.65-0.70
- p0x2 (green→stop): 0.60 (champion only has this as 4th transition)
- p1x0 (gray→GoToColor): 0.15-0.35, sweet spot ~0.25-0.30
- p1x1 (green→GoToColor): 0.45-0.70, sweet spot ~0.50-0.60
- p2x0 (blue→GoToColor): 0.70-0.90
- p2x1 (black→stop): 0.55-0.75

**Strategy for Round 4:**
1. Copy champion exactly (elitism)
2. Fine-tune champion parameters around sweet spots
3. Try 4-transition variants with slightly different params
4. Try adding a 4th state (exploration before settling)
5. Try having state 1 also transition on black floor (to stay stopped)
6. Try state 2 going to state 1 directly (stop) instead of state 0

The 2-state versions performed poorly (worst performers), so stick with 3 states.

Let me focus on fine-grained exploitation of the champion topology, plus a few structural explorations.

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 6 results:**

New champion: 2775.55 with lower probabilities than before:
- p0x0=0.68, p0x1=0.58, p0x2=0.53, p0x3=0.04
- p1x0=0.35, p1x1=0.45, p1x2=0.025
- p2x0=0.63, p2x1=0.53

The trend is clear: **lower probabilities** for all transitions are working better. The search has drifted from p~0.75 down toward p~0.68. This suggests robots should be slightly more likely to stay in their current state (less trigger-happy transitions).

**Key observations:**
- Champion: all probs shifted down ~0.07 from previous champion
- #3 (2909, p0x0=0.71) is between old and new champion - intermediate values
- Worst: changing n2x1 condition from C=0 (black floor) to C=1 (gray floor) kills performance (4467!)
- Adding random fire to state 2 (n2x2, C=5, p=0.03) is slightly helpful (2910 in #4)

**Direction:** Continue drifting lower. Try p0x0 in 0.60-0.68, p0x1 in 0.50-0.58, p0x2 in 0.45-0.53.

Also try: what if we go even lower (p~0.5-0.6 range)? And what if we vary p0x3 around 0.04-0.05?

**Strategy:**
1. Copy champion (elitism)
2. Fine-tune around new champion's values, going both lower and slightly higher
3. Try crossing champion with #3 (intermediate values)
4. Try a variant with p0x3=0.05 (slightly higher random fire)
5. Some exploration with different parameter ranges

## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 8 results:**

The champion (2775.55) still holds. The best performers this round are very close (2780-2820), suggesting we're near a local optimum.

**Key observations:**
- #1 (2780): Champion params + extra random fire to state 2 (n2x2, C=5, p=0.035) → nearly tied!
- #2 (2782): p0x2=0.51, p0x3=0.042, p1x0=0.36, p1x1=0.44, p2x0=0.61, p2x1=0.51
- #3 (2806): p0x0=0.66, lower params across the board
- #4 (2813): p0x0=0.67, p0x2=0.52, p0x3=0.041, p1x0=0.36, p1x1=0.44
- #5 (2820): p0x2=0.50, similar to #4

**Worst observations:**
- p0x3=0.045 with p0x2=0.51 (too high random fire + lower green prob) = 2907
- p0x2=0.51 + extra random fire to state 2 = 2924 (but p0x3=0.04 not 0.042!)
- Interesting: p0x2=0.51 with p0x3=0.042 (2782) works, but p0x2=0.51 with p0x3=0.040 + state2 random (2924) doesn't

The key insight: the random fire in state 2 (from #1 at 2780) combined with champion params is nearly as good as champion. Let me try combining this with the lower-probability variants.

**Sweet spots:**
- p0x0: 0.66-0.68
- p0x1: 0.56-0.58
- p0x2: 0.50-0.53
- p0x3: 0.040-0.043
- p1x0: 0.35-0.37
- p1x1: 0.43-0.45
- p1x2: 0.025-0.026
- p2x0: 0.61-0.63
- p2x1: 0.51-0.53

**Strategy:** Focus on very tight parameter variations around the champion and top performers. Try combining the random-fire-in-state-2 trick with different base params.

- Worst: p0x2=0.51 (too low), p0x2=0.52 with p0x3=0.041 (slightly off)

**This is the FINAL round (10).** I need to maximize the chance of beating the champion.

1. Copy champion (required)
2. Combine #1's extra-blue-transition with #2's slightly lower p0x3/p1x0/p1x1

6. Keep champion params but vary p2x2 (the promising 3rd transition in state 2)

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
OBJECTIVE. At every simulation step the loop function adds +1 to the score for each robot that is NOT inside the target region. The score is MINIMISED, so a lower score means the swarm reached the region earlier and more robots stayed in it.

THE TARGET REGION is the upper strip of the arena. It is NOT identified by a unique floor colour: the floor is BLACK in BOTH the upper and the lower strip, and GRAY in between. Only the UPPER black strip is rewarded. Sitting on the lower black strip scores exactly as badly as standing in the middle of the arena.

HOW TO TELL THE TWO STRIPS APART. At the start of the run the arena walls light up: the two walls bordering the REWARDED upper strip are BLUE, the two walls bordering the penalised lower strip are GREEN. Colour is the only reliable cue.

CONSEQUENCE FOR THE FSM. A black-floor condition is useful to detect that a strip has been reached, but on its own it sends half the swarm to the wrong strip. It must be combined with colour-driven motion - move toward BLUE, or move away from GREEN - to reach the correct one. Once inside, robots should stay rather than keep exploring, since every step spent outside costs a point. Clustering tightly is NOT rewarded: only being inside counts.

# 2. THE ARENA FOR THIS RUN
The arena is a regular polygon with 6 walls, inner radius 0.84 m. 18 e-puck robots start scattered uniformly in a square of half-width 0.62 m around the centre, with random orientations. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.54 m: neighbour-count conditions only see robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).
For reference, the published setup uses 20 robots and a range of 0.50 m, so thresholds tuned for that setup may need adjusting here.

# 3. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 4. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=6  Spiral        -> traces an expanding spiral
            requires --spd<I>  integer in [1..100]  (spiral speed)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=10  FlockCenter   -> moves toward the centre of the group
            requires --fc<I>  integer in [1..100]  (flocking gain)
  B=11  ZigZag        -> moves in a zigzag pattern
            requires --zfr<I>  integer in [1..100]  (zigzag frequency)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 5. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 6. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
